So I keep chasing little pieces—songs he liked, people who should be called, how the displays ought to look, even thinking about trips for it—and then the whole day’s gone.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside. The garden’s a mess, and I haven’t been with my walking group. Funny thing, those tomatoes were actually doing pretty well this year before I got caught up in all this. But the memorial just keeps pulling me back, like there’s always one more thing I need to get right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So I keep chasing little pieces—songs he liked, people who should be called, how the displays ought to look, even thinking about trips for it—and then the whole day’s gone.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside. The garden’s a mess, and I haven’t been with my walking group. Funny thing, those tomatoes were actually doing pretty well this year before I got caught up in all this. But the memorial just keeps pulling me back, like there’s always one more thing I need to get right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the memorial all the time, even when you're trying to do other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s about right. I’ll be trying to do something else, and my mind just jumps—like, one minute I’m thinking about a display case, then suddenly I’m wondering if I should drive out and find this cousin nobody’s talked to in years, then I’ve got some old ballad verse stuck in my head.\n\nAnd I try to write it down so I can make sense of it, but it won’t stay in order. It’s like the thoughts are moving faster than my hand. Overwhelmed… yes, I think that’s fair. It started out as just a memorial for my brother, something simple, but now most days I’m chasing down songs, calling people, figuring out displays, even thinking about trips for it.

Work, no—I’m not working now, so it’s not that. But with people, yes… it has caused trouble.

I’ve missed my walking group and the book club, and the garden’s gone all wild—my wife keeps pointing that out, the tomatoes especially, though they were never as good as my father’s used to be. And at the senior center, I think I’ve upset a few people because I keep turning every conversation back to the memorial display. I don’t mean to, but someone says one thing about a song or a photo and I’m off again.

My wife says our whole day ends up being about calming me down or steering me away from another phone call, or some trip I suddenly think I need to make, or an argument over how the display should be. So… yes, it’s been hard on things between us and other people.

My wife says the garden’s been left, and I haven’t been seeing the walking group, which I used to like—there’s a fellow there who always tells the same joke about his knees—but anyway, yes, it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to, and it does feel like everything else has been pushed aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of the memorial yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say control every bit of it… but I do keep taking it back onto myself. Like, if someone else says they’ll call a person or pick a song, I start thinking, well, maybe they won’t remember the right version, or they won’t know what my brother would’ve liked.\n\nSo then I’m the one chasing down the songs, calling folks, planning the displays, even thinking about trips for it. And then the whole day’s gone. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been with the walking group in a while—there’s a man there who always brings peppermint candies, funny little habit—but yes, I suppose I’ve pushed most everything else aside for this.\n\nDoctor: Has your focus on the memorial caused problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Work, no—I’m not working now, so it’s not that. But with people, yes… it has caused trouble.\n\nI’ve missed my walking group and the book club, and the garden’s gone all wild—my wife keeps pointing that out, the tomatoes especially, though they were never as good as my father’s used to be. And at the senior center, I think I’ve upset a few people because I keep turning every conversation back to the memorial display. I don’t mean to, but someone says one thing about a song or a photo and I’m off again.\n\nMy wife says our whole day ends up being about calming me down or steering me away from another phone call, or some trip I suddenly think I need to make, or an argument over how the display should be. So… yes, it’s been hard on things between us and other people.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… yes, I think it is. I don’t like saying that, because the memorial feels important, you know, it’s for my brother, and he loved those old songs—there was one he used to hum when we were kids, though I can’t remember if it was from the radio or something my mother sang.

My wife says our days are all about settling me down or stopping me from making another call, or planning another trip, or getting into some argument about how it should look. So… yes, I suppose it’s affecting me more than I wanted to admit.

It started as something small, just a nice thing, you know, a few songs he liked—he had this old record he used to play, drove everyone nuts—but now it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to.\n\nMost days I’m calling people, tracking down music, planning displays, thinking about trips and who should be there and how it ought to look. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been going with my walking group, and… well, she says I’ve pushed just about everything else aside for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything about the memorial has to be just right?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do, or… maybe not “perfect,” but it feels like if I leave something out, I’m letting him down. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I suppose anybody thinks about what they could’ve done differently when someone dies, don’t they? But I don’t have some one thing, if that’s what you mean, like something I did or didn’t do.

It’s more… I keep thinking the memorial has to be right. Like if the songs are wrong, or the photos are in the wrong order, then people won’t remember him the way they should. He had this laugh—my brother—and there was a picture from years ago, I think at a picnic, or maybe my cousin’s place, and I keep trying to find it.

So guilt… maybe worry, maybe pressure. I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.

So… yes, it’s been hard on things between us and other people.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your efforts on the memorial are taking a toll on your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. I don’t like saying that, because the memorial feels important, you know, it’s for my brother, and he loved those old songs—there was one he used to hum when we were kids, though I can’t remember if it was from the radio or something my mother sang.\n\nBut yes, it’s taking a toll. I’ve missed the walking group, and book club too. The garden’s been let go, my wife keeps saying it’s getting away from us. And at the senior center, I think I’ve worn people out because everything I talk about turns into the memorial display somehow.\n\nMy wife says our days are all about settling me down or stopping me from making another call, or planning another trip, or getting into some argument about how it should look. So… yes, I suppose it’s affecting me more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about something related to your brother's death that's making you put so much into the memorial?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I suppose anybody thinks about what they could’ve done differently when someone dies, don’t they? But I don’t have some one thing, if that’s what you mean, like something I did or didn’t do.\n\nIt’s more… I keep thinking the memorial has to be right. Like if the songs are wrong, or the photos are in the wrong order, then people won’t remember him the way they should. He had this laugh—my brother—and there was a picture from years ago, I think at a picnic, or maybe my cousin’s place, and I keep trying to find it.\n\nSo guilt… maybe worry, maybe pressure. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Afraid I’ll forget… yes, maybe that’s part of it. It’s more that the whole thing has just grown and grown. I started with wanting a nice memorial for my brother, but now I’m spending most days chasing down the right songs, calling people, thinking about photos and displays, and then I start planning some trip to find one thing or talk to someone.

And you know, my brother had opinions about music—he’d say the radio was all noise after a certain year, which my wife says I’ve started saying too much lately. But yes, I keep feeling like if I leave something out, it won’t be right somehow.

My wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside—the garden, my walking group, all of it. So I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of forgetting, but I can’t seem to stop trying to get every part of it down.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess… it’s this memorial I’m putting together for my brother. It started as something small, just a nice thing, you know, a few songs he liked—he had this old record he used to play, drove everyone nuts—but now it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to.\n\nMost days I’m calling people, tracking down music, planning displays, thinking about trips and who should be there and how it ought to look. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been going with my walking group, and… well, she says I’ve pushed just about everything else aside for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything about the memorial has to be just right?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do, or… maybe not “perfect,” but it feels like if I leave something out, I’m letting him down. So I keep chasing little pieces—songs he liked, people who should be called, how the displays ought to look, even thinking about trips for it—and then the whole day’s gone.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside. The garden’s a mess, and I haven’t been with my walking group. Funny thing, those tomatoes were actually doing pretty well this year before I got caught up in all this. But the memorial just keeps pulling me back, like there’s always one more thing I need to get right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the memorial all the time, even when you're trying to do other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s about right. I’ll be trying to do something else, and my mind just jumps—like, one minute I’m thinking about a display case, then suddenly I’m wondering if I should drive out and find this cousin nobody’s talked to in years, then I’ve got some old ballad verse stuck in my head.\n\nAnd I try to write it down so I can make sense of it, but it won’t stay in order. It’s like the thoughts are moving faster than my hand. Then I remember my brother used to hum that tune when he was fixing the radio—he was always taking things apart, drove my mother crazy—and then I’m off on that, and I’ve forgotten what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or stressed by all the work you're putting into the memorial?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed… yes, I think that’s fair. It started out as just a memorial for my brother, something simple, but now most days I’m chasing down songs, calling people, figuring out displays, even thinking about trips for it.\n\nAnd then I look up and the day’s gone. My wife says the garden’s been left, and I haven’t been seeing the walking group, which I used to like—there’s a fellow there who always tells the same joke about his knees—but anyway, yes, it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to, and it does feel like everything else has been pushed aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of the memorial yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say control every bit of it… but I do keep taking it back onto myself. Like, if someone else says they’ll call a person or pick a song, I start thinking, well, maybe they won’t remember the right version, or they won’t know what my brother would’ve liked.\n\nSo then I’m the one chasing down the songs, calling folks, planning the displays, even thinking about trips for it. And then the whole day’s gone. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been with the walking group in a while—there’s a man there who always brings peppermint candies, funny little habit—but yes, I suppose I’ve pushed most everything else aside for this.\n\nDoctor: Has your focus on the memorial caused problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Work, no—I’m not working now, so it’s not that. But with people, yes… it has caused trouble.\n\nI’ve missed my walking group and the book club, and the garden’s gone all wild—my wife keeps pointing that out, the tomatoes especially, though they were never as good as my father’s used to be. And at the senior center, I think I’ve upset a few people because I keep turning every conversation back to the memorial display. I don’t mean to, but someone says one thing about a song or a photo and I’m off again.\n\nMy wife says our whole day ends up being about calming me down or steering me away from another phone call, or some trip I suddenly think I need to make, or an argument over how the display should be. So… yes, it’s been hard on things between us and other people.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your efforts on the memorial are taking a toll on your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. I’ve missed the walking group, and book club too. The garden’s been let go, my wife keeps saying it’s getting away from us. And at the senior center, I think I’ve worn people out because everything I talk about turns into the memorial display somehow.\n\nMy wife says our days are all about settling me down or stopping me from making another call, or planning another trip, or getting into some argument about how it should look. I mean, I suppose anybody thinks about what they could’ve done differently when someone dies, don’t they? But I don’t have some one thing, if that’s what you mean, like something I did or didn’t do.\n\nIt’s more… I keep thinking the memorial has to be right. Like if the songs are wrong, or the photos are in the wrong order, then people won’t remember him the way they should. He had this laugh—my brother—and there was a picture from years ago, I think at a picnic, or maybe my cousin’s place, and I keep trying to find it.\n\nSo guilt… maybe worry, maybe pressure. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe… maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. What brings me in is more that the memorial has gotten bigger than I ever meant it to. I started with just wanting something nice for my brother, and then suddenly I’m spending most days chasing down songs, calling folks, planning the display, thinking I should take a trip to find this or that.

And my wife says I’ve let everything else slide—the garden, my walking group… I used to like that walking group, there’s a fellow there who always talks about baseball, even when nobody asks him to. But I haven’t been going. It’s like everything keeps getting pulled back to the memorial.

So yes, I suppose I’m afraid of losing pieces of him, but mostly I just can’t seem to stop adding to it and working on it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess… it’s this memorial I’m putting together for my brother. It started as something small, just a nice thing, you know, a few songs he liked—he had this old record he used to play, drove everyone nuts—but now it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to.\n\nMost days I’m calling people, tracking down music, planning displays, thinking about trips and who should be there and how it ought to look. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been going with my walking group, and… well, she says I’ve pushed just about everything else aside for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything about the memorial has to be just right?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do, or… maybe not “perfect,” but it feels like if I leave something out, I’m letting him down. The garden’s a mess, and I haven’t been with my walking group. Funny thing, those tomatoes were actually doing pretty well this year before I got caught up in all this. I’ll be trying to do something else, and my mind just jumps—like, one minute I’m thinking about a display case, then suddenly I’m wondering if I should drive out and find this cousin nobody’s talked to in years, then I’ve got some old ballad verse stuck in my head.\n\nAnd I try to write it down so I can make sense of it, but it won’t stay in order. It’s like the thoughts are moving faster than my hand. Then I remember my brother used to hum that tune when he was fixing the radio—he was always taking things apart, drove my mother crazy—and then I’m off on that, and I’ve forgotten what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or stressed by all the work you're putting into the memorial?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed… yes, I think that’s fair. It started out as just a memorial for my brother, something simple, but now most days I’m chasing down songs, calling people, figuring out displays, even thinking about trips for it.\n\nAnd then I look up and the day’s gone. My wife says the garden’s been left, and I haven’t been seeing the walking group, which I used to like—there’s a fellow there who always tells the same joke about his knees—but anyway, yes, it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to, and it does feel like everything else has been pushed aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of the memorial yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say control every bit of it… but I do keep taking it back onto myself. Like, if someone else says they’ll call a person or pick a song, I start thinking, well, maybe they won’t remember the right version, or they won’t know what my brother would’ve liked.\n\nSo then I’m the one chasing down the songs, calling folks, planning the displays, even thinking about trips for it. But with people, yes… it has caused trouble.\n\nI’ve missed my walking group and the book club, and the garden’s gone all wild—my wife keeps pointing that out, the tomatoes especially, though they were never as good as my father’s used to be. So… yes, it’s been hard on things between us and other people.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your efforts on the memorial are taking a toll on your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. I don’t like saying that, because the memorial feels important, you know, it’s for my brother, and he loved those old songs—there was one he used to hum when we were kids, though I can’t remember if it was from the radio or something my mother sang.\n\nBut yes, it’s taking a toll. It started as something small, just a nice thing, you know, a few songs he liked—he had this old record he used to play, drove everyone nuts—but now it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to.\n\nMost days I’m calling people, tracking down music, planning displays, thinking about trips and who should be there and how it ought to look. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been going with my walking group, and… well, she says I’ve pushed just about everything else aside for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything about the memorial has to be just right?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do, or… maybe not “perfect,” but it feels like if I leave something out, I’m letting him down. So I keep chasing little pieces—songs he liked, people who should be called, how the displays ought to look, even thinking about trips for it—and then the whole day’s gone.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside. The garden’s a mess, and I haven’t been with my walking group. Funny thing, those tomatoes were actually doing pretty well this year before I got caught up in all this. But the memorial just keeps pulling me back, like there’s always one more thing I need to get right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the memorial all the time, even when you're trying to do other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s about right. I’ll be trying to do something else, and my mind just jumps—like, one minute I’m thinking about a display case, then suddenly I’m wondering if I should drive out and find this cousin nobody’s talked to in years, then I’ve got some old ballad verse stuck in my head.\n\nAnd I try to write it down so I can make sense of it, but it won’t stay in order. It’s like the thoughts are moving faster than my hand. Then I remember my brother used to hum that tune when he was fixing the radio—he was always taking things apart, drove my mother crazy—and then I’m off on that, and I’ve forgotten what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or stressed by all the work you're putting into the memorial?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed… yes, I think that’s fair. It started out as just a memorial for my brother, something simple, but now most days I’m chasing down songs, calling people, figuring out displays, even thinking about trips for it.\n\nAnd then I look up and the day’s gone. My wife says the garden’s been left, and I haven’t been seeing the walking group, which I used to like—there’s a fellow there who always tells the same joke about his knees—but anyway, yes, it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to, and it does feel like everything else has been pushed aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of the memorial yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say control every bit of it… but I do keep taking it back onto myself. Like, if someone else says they’ll call a person or pick a song, I start thinking, well, maybe they won’t remember the right version, or they won’t know what my brother would’ve liked.\n\nSo then I’m the one chasing down the songs, calling folks, planning the displays, even thinking about trips for it. And then the whole day’s gone. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been with the walking group in a while—there’s a man there who always brings peppermint candies, funny little habit—but yes, I suppose I’ve pushed most everything else aside for this.\n\nDoctor: Has your focus on the memorial caused problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Work, no—I’m not working now, so it’s not that. But with people, yes… it has caused trouble.\n\nI’ve missed my walking group and the book club, and the garden’s gone all wild—my wife keeps pointing that out, the tomatoes especially, though they were never as good as my father’s used to be. And at the senior center, I think I’ve upset a few people because I keep turning every conversation back to the memorial display. I don’t mean to, but someone says one thing about a song or a photo and I’m off again.\n\nMy wife says our whole day ends up being about calming me down or steering me away from another phone call, or some trip I suddenly think I need to make, or an argument over how the display should be. So… yes, it’s been hard on things between us and other people.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your efforts on the memorial are taking a toll on your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. I don’t like saying that, because the memorial feels important, you know, it’s for my brother, and he loved those old songs—there was one he used to hum when we were kids, though I can’t remember if it was from the radio or something my mother sang.\n\nBut yes, it’s taking a toll. I’ve missed the walking group, and book club too. The garden’s been let go, my wife keeps saying it’s getting away from us. And at the senior center, I think I’ve worn people out because everything I talk about turns into the memorial display somehow.\n\nMy wife says our days are all about settling me down or stopping me from making another call, or planning another trip, or getting into some argument about how it should look. So… yes, I suppose it’s affecting me more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about something related to your brother's death that's making you put so much into the memorial?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I suppose anybody thinks about what they could’ve done differently when someone dies, don’t they? But I don’t have some one thing, if that’s what you mean, like something I did or didn’t do.\n\nIt’s more… I keep thinking the memorial has to be right. Like if the songs are wrong, or the photos are in the wrong order, then people won’t remember him the way they should. He had this laugh—my brother—and there was a picture from years ago, I think at a picnic, or maybe my cousin’s place, and I keep trying to find it.\n\nSo guilt… maybe worry, maybe pressure. I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't include every detail in the memorial, you will forget something important about your brother?\n\nPatient: Afraid I’ll forget… yes, maybe that’s part of it. It’s more that the whole thing has just grown and grown. I started with wanting a nice memorial for my brother, but now I’m spending most days chasing down the right songs, calling people, thinking about photos and displays, and then I start planning some trip to find one thing or talk to someone.\n\nAnd you know, my brother had opinions about music—he’d say the radio was all noise after a certain year, which my wife says I’ve started saying too much lately. But yes, I keep feeling like if I leave something out, it won’t be right somehow.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside—the garden, my walking group, all of it. So I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of forgetting, but I can’t seem to stop trying to get every part of it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all this for the memorial because you're afraid of forgetting your brother?\n\nPatient: Maybe… maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. What brings me in is more that the memorial has gotten bigger than I ever meant it to. I started with just wanting something nice for my brother, and then suddenly I’m spending most days chasing down songs, calling folks, planning the display, thinking I should take a trip to find this or that.\n\nAnd my wife says I’ve let everything else slide—the garden, my walking group… I used to like that walking group, there’s a fellow there who always talks about baseball, even when nobody asks him to. But I haven’t been going. I’ll try to sit down and think, alright, just make a list, keep it sensible, and then my mind is off—first it’s the display case, then I’m wondering if I should drive out to find my cousin because he might have that one photo, then some line from an old ballad pops in, and I’m thinking maybe that should be read aloud.\n\nIt doesn’t stay in a straight line, if you know what I mean. My brother used to tease me about making lists and then losing the list—he had this little notebook he carried, always said I should get one. I don’t know that it’s perfect, exactly… I mean, maybe I want it right, but the bigger thing is I can’t stop the thoughts once they start.

But I don’t have some one thing, if that’s what you mean, like something I did or didn’t do.\n\nIt’s more… I keep thinking the memorial has to be right. Like if the songs are wrong, or the photos are in the wrong order, then people won’t remember him the way they should. He had this laugh—my brother—and there was a picture from years ago, I think at a picnic, or maybe my cousin’s place, and I keep trying to find it.\n\nSo guilt… maybe worry, maybe pressure. I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't include every detail in the memorial, you will forget something important about your brother?\n\nPatient: Afraid I’ll forget… yes, maybe that’s part of it. It’s more that the whole thing has just grown and grown. I started with wanting a nice memorial for my brother, but now I’m spending most days chasing down the right songs, calling people, thinking about photos and displays, and then I start planning some trip to find one thing or talk to someone.\n\nAnd you know, my brother had opinions about music—he’d say the radio was all noise after a certain year, which my wife says I’ve started saying too much lately. But yes, I keep feeling like if I leave something out, it won’t be right somehow.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside—the garden, my walking group, all of it. So I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of forgetting, but I can’t seem to stop trying to get every part of it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all this for the memorial because you're afraid of forgetting your brother?\n\nPatient: Maybe… maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. What brings me in is more that the memorial has gotten bigger than I ever meant it to. I started with just wanting something nice for my brother, and then suddenly I’m spending most days chasing down songs, calling folks, planning the display, thinking I should take a trip to find this or that.\n\nAnd my wife says I’ve let everything else slide—the garden, my walking group… I used to like that walking group, there’s a fellow there who always talks about baseball, even when nobody asks him to. But I haven’t been going. It’s like everything keeps getting pulled back to the memorial.\n\nSo yes, I suppose I’m afraid of losing pieces of him, but mostly I just can’t seem to stop adding to it and working on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about the memorial even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s a good way of putting it. I’ll try to sit down and think, alright, just make a list, keep it sensible, and then my mind is off—first it’s the display case, then I’m wondering if I should drive out to find my cousin because he might have that one photo, then some line from an old ballad pops in, and I’m thinking maybe that should be read aloud.\n\nIt doesn’t stay in a straight line, if you know what I mean. My brother used to tease me about making lists and then losing the list—he had this little notebook he carried, always said I should get one. But now even when I write things down, it’s like I can’t get the thoughts in order fast enough. They keep jumping ahead of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to make the memorial perfect, or is it just that you can't stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s perfect, exactly… I mean, maybe I want it right, but the bigger thing is I can’t stop the thoughts once they start.\n\nMy mind jumps all over. One minute I’m thinking about a display case, then I’m thinking I should take a road trip to find my cousin because he might know something, then some old ballad verse comes into my head and I think, no, that has to be in there too. I think it’s more that I can’t seem to stop thinking about it. I mean, I do want it to be good, of course, it’s for my brother—but it’s not like I sat down and said, “This has to be enormous.”

It’s more like my mind keeps jumping. One minute I’m thinking about a display case, then I’m thinking maybe I ought to take a road trip to find my cousin because he might have something important, and then suddenly there’s some old ballad verse in my head—my mother used to hum songs while she cooked, not always the same words, she’d mix them up—and then I think, well, maybe that belongs in the memorial too.

And when I try to write it in order, I can’t slow it down enough. By the time I’ve written one thing, I’m already three ideas past it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess… it’s this memorial I’m putting together for my brother. It started as something small, just a nice thing, you know, a few songs he liked—he had this old record he used to play, drove everyone nuts—but now it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to.\n\nMost days I’m calling people, tracking down music, planning displays, thinking about trips and who should be there and how it ought to look. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been going with my walking group, and… well, she says I’ve pushed just about everything else aside for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything about the memorial has to be just right?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do, or… maybe not “perfect,” but it feels like if I leave something out, I’m letting him down. So I keep chasing little pieces—songs he liked, people who should be called, how the displays ought to look, even thinking about trips for it—and then the whole day’s gone.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside. The garden’s a mess, and I haven’t been with my walking group. Funny thing, those tomatoes were actually doing pretty well this year before I got caught up in all this. But the memorial just keeps pulling me back, like there’s always one more thing I need to get right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the memorial all the time, even when you're trying to do other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s about right. I’ll be trying to do something else, and my mind just jumps—like, one minute I’m thinking about a display case, then suddenly I’m wondering if I should drive out and find this cousin nobody’s talked to in years, then I’ve got some old ballad verse stuck in my head.\n\nAnd I try to write it down so I can make sense of it, but it won’t stay in order. It’s like the thoughts are moving faster than my hand. Then I remember my brother used to hum that tune when he was fixing the radio—he was always taking things apart, drove my mother crazy—and then I’m off on that, and I’ve forgotten what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or stressed by all the work you're putting into the memorial?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed… yes, I think that’s fair. It started out as just a memorial for my brother, something simple, but now most days I’m chasing down songs, calling people, figuring out displays, even thinking about trips for it.\n\nAnd then I look up and the day’s gone. My wife says the garden’s been left, and I haven’t been seeing the walking group, which I used to like—there’s a fellow there who always tells the same joke about his knees—but anyway, yes, it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to, and it does feel like everything else has been pushed aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of the memorial yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say control every bit of it… but I do keep taking it back onto myself. Like, if someone else says they’ll call a person or pick a song, I start thinking, well, maybe they won’t remember the right version, or they won’t know what my brother would’ve liked.\n\nSo then I’m the one chasing down the songs, calling folks, planning the displays, even thinking about trips for it. And then the whole day’s gone. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been with the walking group in a while—there’s a man there who always brings peppermint candies, funny little habit—but yes, I suppose I’ve pushed most everything else aside for this.\n\nDoctor: Has your focus on the memorial caused problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Work, no—I’m not working now, so it’s not that. But with people, yes… it has caused trouble.\n\nI’ve missed my walking group and the book club, and the garden’s gone all wild—my wife keeps pointing that out, the tomatoes especially, though they were never as good as my father’s used to be. And at the senior center, I think I’ve upset a few people because I keep turning every conversation back to the memorial display. I don’t mean to, but someone says one thing about a song or a photo and I’m off again.\n\nMy wife says our whole day ends up being about calming me down or steering me away from another phone call, or some trip I suddenly think I need to make, or an argument over how the display should be. So… yes, it’s been hard on things between us and other people.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your efforts on the memorial are taking a toll on your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. I don’t like saying that, because the memorial feels important, you know, it’s for my brother, and he loved those old songs—there was one he used to hum when we were kids, though I can’t remember if it was from the radio or something my mother sang.\n\nBut yes, it’s taking a toll. I’ve missed the walking group, and book club too. The garden’s been let go, my wife keeps saying it’s getting away from us. And at the senior center, I think I’ve worn people out because everything I talk about turns into the memorial display somehow.\n\nMy wife says our days are all about settling me down or stopping me from making another call, or planning another trip, or getting into some argument about how it should look. So… yes, I suppose it’s affecting me more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about something related to your brother's death that's making you put so much into the memorial?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I suppose anybody thinks about what they could’ve done differently when someone dies, don’t they? But I don’t have some one thing, if that’s what you mean, like something I did or didn’t do.\n\nIt’s more… I keep thinking the memorial has to be right. Like if the songs are wrong, or the photos are in the wrong order, then people won’t remember him the way they should. He had this laugh—my brother—and there was a picture from years ago, I think at a picnic, or maybe my cousin’s place, and I keep trying to find it.\n\nSo guilt… maybe worry, maybe pressure. I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't include every detail in the memorial, you will forget something important about your brother?\n\nPatient: Afraid I’ll forget… yes, maybe that’s part of it. It’s more that the whole thing has just grown and grown. I started with wanting a nice memorial for my brother, but now I’m spending most days chasing down the right songs, calling people, thinking about photos and displays, and then I start planning some trip to find one thing or talk to someone.\n\nAnd you know, my brother had opinions about music—he’d say the radio was all noise after a certain year, which my wife says I’ve started saying too much lately. But yes, I keep feeling like if I leave something out, it won’t be right somehow.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside—the garden, my walking group, all of it. So I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of forgetting, but I can’t seem to stop trying to get every part of it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all this for the memorial because you're afraid of forgetting your brother?\n\nPatient: Maybe… maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. What brings me in is more that the memorial has gotten bigger than I ever meant it to. I started with just wanting something nice for my brother, and then suddenly I’m spending most days chasing down songs, calling folks, planning the display, thinking I should take a trip to find this or that.\n\nAnd my wife says I’ve let everything else slide—the garden, my walking group… I used to like that walking group, there’s a fellow there who always talks about baseball, even when nobody asks him to. But I haven’t been going. It’s like everything keeps getting pulled back to the memorial.\n\nSo yes, I suppose I’m afraid of losing pieces of him, but mostly I just can’t seem to stop adding to it and working on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about the memorial even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s a good way of putting it. I’ll try to sit down and think, alright, just make a list, keep it sensible, and then my mind is off—first it’s the display case, then I’m wondering if I should drive out to find my cousin because he might have that one photo, then some line from an old ballad pops in, and I’m thinking maybe that should be read aloud.\n\nIt doesn’t stay in a straight line, if you know what I mean. My brother used to tease me about making lists and then losing the list—he had this little notebook he carried, always said I should get one. But now even when I write things down, it’s like I can’t get the thoughts in order fast enough. They keep jumping ahead of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to make the memorial perfect, or is it just that you can't stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s perfect, exactly… I mean, maybe I want it right, but the bigger thing is I can’t stop the thoughts once they start.\n\nMy mind jumps all over. One minute I’m thinking about a display case, then I’m thinking I should take a road trip to find my cousin because he might know something, then some old ballad verse comes into my head and I think, no, that has to be in there too. And then I try to write it down, but by the time I’ve got the pen, I’m already on the next idea.\n\nIt’s like I can’t slow it down enough to put it in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing all this work for the memorial because you genuinely believe it needs to be this extensive, or because you just can't seem to stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t seem to stop thinking about it. I mean, I do want it to be good, of course, it’s for my brother—but it’s not like I sat down and said, “This has to be enormous.”\n\nIt’s more like my mind keeps jumping. One minute I’m thinking about a display case, then I’m thinking maybe I ought to take a road trip to find my cousin because he might have something important, and then suddenly there’s some old ballad verse in my head—my mother used to hum songs while she cooked, not always the same words, she’d mix them up—and then I think, well, maybe that belongs in the memorial too.\n\nAnd when I try to write it in order, I can’t slow it down enough. By the time I’ve written one thing, I’m already three ideas past it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

---------- turn 14 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S019\n  name: Delusions\n  description: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\n  manifestation: I know it sounds odd, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs—they’ve got warnings tucked in them for me, and when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I still know what I heard in those words.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: Sometimes when I’m sorting his song sheets at the kitchen table, I hear my brother’s voice clear as day humming behind me or telling me which tune comes next, even though nobody else in the house hears a thing.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: I’ll feel lit up and full of purpose one minute, like the whole town is finally going to understand our family, and then I snap at my wife or someone at the senior center if they slow me down or question the display.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I can’t seem to sit in my chair like I used to; I’m up hauling boxes, making calls, rearranging papers, walking room to room, feeling like my body has more steam in it than a man my age ought to have.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: At the senior center meetings I start explaining the songs and the quilts and the photos, and before I know it I’ve talked over three people and my pastor is gently saying, ‘Aaron, let’s let someone else speak.’\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My mind keeps jumping from one idea to the next—one minute it’s a display case, then a road trip to find a cousin, then a verse from a ballad—and I can’t slow it down enough to write it in order.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that this memorial for my brother has gotten bigger than I meant it to, and I’ve been spending most days chasing down songs, calling folks, planning displays and trips, until my wife says I’ve pushed aside the garden, walking group, and just about everything else.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the manic episode\n  manifestation: Before I got swept up in this memorial work after my brother died, I never heard his voice in the house or felt the songs were sending me personal warnings; those things started along with this burst of energy and nonstop planning.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’ve missed my walking group and book club, let the garden go, and upset friends at the senior center because everything has become about the memorial display. My wife says our days revolve around calming me down or redirecting me from another call, trip, or argument.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two months, ever since my brother passed and I began working on his tribute.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S040\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about the memorial even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s a good way of putting it. My brother used to tease me about making lists and then losing the list—he had this little notebook he carried, always said I should get one. But now even when I write things down, it’s like I can’t get the thoughts in order fast enough. They keep jumping ahead of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to make the memorial perfect, or is it just that you can't stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s perfect, exactly… I mean, maybe I want it right, but the bigger thing is I can’t stop the thoughts once they start.\n\nMy mind jumps all over. I mean, I do want it to be good, of course, it’s for my brother—but it’s not like I sat down and said, “This has to be enormous.”\n\nIt’s more like my mind keeps jumping. One minute I’m thinking about a display case, then I’m thinking maybe I ought to take a road trip to find my cousin because he might have something important, and then suddenly there’s some old ballad verse in my head—my mother used to hum songs while she cooked, not always the same words, she’d mix them up—and then I think, well, maybe that belongs in the memorial too.\n\nAnd when I try to write it in order, I can’t slow it down enough. By the time I’ve written one thing, I’m already three ideas past it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're doing these things for the memorial because you feel compelled to, or because you genuinely want to?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

I’ll try to sit down and think, alright, just make a list, keep it sensible, and then my mind is off—first it’s the display case, then I’m wondering if I should drive out to find my cousin because he might have that one photo, then some line from an old ballad pops in, and I’m thinking maybe that should be read aloud.\n\nIt doesn’t stay in a straight line, if you know what I mean. But now even when I write things down, it’s like I can’t get the thoughts in order fast enough. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose it’s both, but… more like I feel pulled along by it now. I did want to do something nice for my brother, genuinely. That part’s real.

But what brings me in is that it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to. Most days I’m chasing down old songs, calling people, thinking about displays, whether I should take trips to find things… and then the day’s gone. My wife says I’ve pushed aside the garden, and my walking group, and just about everything else.

I don’t know if “compelled” is the right word, but it doesn’t feel like I can just set it down and say, alright, enough for today.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess… it’s this memorial I’m putting together for my brother. It started as something small, just a nice thing, you know, a few songs he liked—he had this old record he used to play, drove everyone nuts—but now it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to.\n\nMost days I’m calling people, tracking down music, planning displays, thinking about trips and who should be there and how it ought to look. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been going with my walking group, and… well, she says I’ve pushed just about everything else aside for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything about the memorial has to be just right?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do, or… maybe not “perfect,” but it feels like if I leave something out, I’m letting him down. So I keep chasing little pieces—songs he liked, people who should be called, how the displays ought to look, even thinking about trips for it—and then the whole day’s gone.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside. The garden’s a mess, and I haven’t been with my walking group. Funny thing, those tomatoes were actually doing pretty well this year before I got caught up in all this. But the memorial just keeps pulling me back, like there’s always one more thing I need to get right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the memorial all the time, even when you're trying to do other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s about right. I’ll be trying to do something else, and my mind just jumps—like, one minute I’m thinking about a display case, then suddenly I’m wondering if I should drive out and find this cousin nobody’s talked to in years, then I’ve got some old ballad verse stuck in my head.\n\nAnd I try to write it down so I can make sense of it, but it won’t stay in order. It’s like the thoughts are moving faster than my hand. Then I remember my brother used to hum that tune when he was fixing the radio—he was always taking things apart, drove my mother crazy—and then I’m off on that, and I’ve forgotten what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or stressed by all the work you're putting into the memorial?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed… yes, I think that’s fair. It started out as just a memorial for my brother, something simple, but now most days I’m chasing down songs, calling people, figuring out displays, even thinking about trips for it.\n\nAnd then I look up and the day’s gone. My wife says the garden’s been left, and I haven’t been seeing the walking group, which I used to like—there’s a fellow there who always tells the same joke about his knees—but anyway, yes, it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to, and it does feel like everything else has been pushed aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of the memorial yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say control every bit of it… but I do keep taking it back onto myself. Like, if someone else says they’ll call a person or pick a song, I start thinking, well, maybe they won’t remember the right version, or they won’t know what my brother would’ve liked.\n\nSo then I’m the one chasing down the songs, calling folks, planning the displays, even thinking about trips for it. And then the whole day’s gone. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been with the walking group in a while—there’s a man there who always brings peppermint candies, funny little habit—but yes, I suppose I’ve pushed most everything else aside for this.\n\nDoctor: Has your focus on the memorial caused problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Work, no—I’m not working now, so it’s not that. But with people, yes… it has caused trouble.\n\nI’ve missed my walking group and the book club, and the garden’s gone all wild—my wife keeps pointing that out, the tomatoes especially, though they were never as good as my father’s used to be. And at the senior center, I think I’ve upset a few people because I keep turning every conversation back to the memorial display. I don’t mean to, but someone says one thing about a song or a photo and I’m off again.\n\nMy wife says our whole day ends up being about calming me down or steering me away from another phone call, or some trip I suddenly think I need to make, or an argument over how the display should be. So… yes, it’s been hard on things between us and other people.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your efforts on the memorial are taking a toll on your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. I don’t like saying that, because the memorial feels important, you know, it’s for my brother, and he loved those old songs—there was one he used to hum when we were kids, though I can’t remember if it was from the radio or something my mother sang.\n\nBut yes, it’s taking a toll. I’ve missed the walking group, and book club too. The garden’s been let go, my wife keeps saying it’s getting away from us. And at the senior center, I think I’ve worn people out because everything I talk about turns into the memorial display somehow.\n\nMy wife says our days are all about settling me down or stopping me from making another call, or planning another trip, or getting into some argument about how it should look. So… yes, I suppose it’s affecting me more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about something related to your brother's death that's making you put so much into the memorial?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I suppose anybody thinks about what they could’ve done differently when someone dies, don’t they? But I don’t have some one thing, if that’s what you mean, like something I did or didn’t do.\n\nIt’s more… I keep thinking the memorial has to be right. Like if the songs are wrong, or the photos are in the wrong order, then people won’t remember him the way they should. He had this laugh—my brother—and there was a picture from years ago, I think at a picnic, or maybe my cousin’s place, and I keep trying to find it.\n\nSo guilt… maybe worry, maybe pressure. I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't include every detail in the memorial, you will forget something important about your brother?\n\nPatient: Afraid I’ll forget… yes, maybe that’s part of it. It’s more that the whole thing has just grown and grown. I started with wanting a nice memorial for my brother, but now I’m spending most days chasing down the right songs, calling people, thinking about photos and displays, and then I start planning some trip to find one thing or talk to someone.\n\nAnd you know, my brother had opinions about music—he’d say the radio was all noise after a certain year, which my wife says I’ve started saying too much lately. But yes, I keep feeling like if I leave something out, it won’t be right somehow.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside—the garden, my walking group, all of it. So I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of forgetting, but I can’t seem to stop trying to get every part of it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all this for the memorial because you're afraid of forgetting your brother?\n\nPatient: Maybe… maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. What brings me in is more that the memorial has gotten bigger than I ever meant it to. I started with just wanting something nice for my brother, and then suddenly I’m spending most days chasing down songs, calling folks, planning the display, thinking I should take a trip to find this or that.\n\nAnd my wife says I’ve let everything else slide—the garden, my walking group… I used to like that walking group, there’s a fellow there who always talks about baseball, even when nobody asks him to. But I haven’t been going. It’s like everything keeps getting pulled back to the memorial.\n\nSo yes, I suppose I’m afraid of losing pieces of him, but mostly I just can’t seem to stop adding to it and working on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about the memorial even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s a good way of putting it. I’ll try to sit down and think, alright, just make a list, keep it sensible, and then my mind is off—first it’s the display case, then I’m wondering if I should drive out to find my cousin because he might have that one photo, then some line from an old ballad pops in, and I’m thinking maybe that should be read aloud.\n\nIt doesn’t stay in a straight line, if you know what I mean. My brother used to tease me about making lists and then losing the list—he had this little notebook he carried, always said I should get one. But now even when I write things down, it’s like I can’t get the thoughts in order fast enough. They keep jumping ahead of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to make the memorial perfect, or is it just that you can't stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s perfect, exactly… I mean, maybe I want it right, but the bigger thing is I can’t stop the thoughts once they start.\n\nMy mind jumps all over. One minute I’m thinking about a display case, then I’m thinking I should take a road trip to find my cousin because he might know something, then some old ballad verse comes into my head and I think, no, that has to be in there too. And then I try to write it down, but by the time I’ve got the pen, I’m already on the next idea.\n\nIt’s like I can’t slow it down enough to put it in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing all this work for the memorial because you genuinely believe it needs to be this extensive, or because you just can't seem to stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t seem to stop thinking about it. One minute I’m thinking about a display case, then I’m thinking maybe I ought to take a road trip to find my cousin because he might have something important, and then suddenly there’s some old ballad verse in my head—my mother used to hum songs while she cooked, not always the same words, she’d mix them up—and then I think, well, maybe that belongs in the memorial too.\n\nAnd when I try to write it in order, I can’t slow it down enough. By the time I’ve written one thing, I’m already three ideas past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things for the memorial because you feel compelled to, or because you genuinely want to?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but… more like I feel pulled along by it now. I did want to do something nice for my brother, genuinely. That part’s real.\n\nBut what brings me in is that it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to. Most days I’m chasing down old songs, calling people, thinking about displays, whether I should take trips to find things… and then the day’s gone. My wife says I’ve pushed aside the garden, and my walking group, and just about everything else.\n\nI don’t know if “compelled” is the right word, but it doesn’t feel like I can just set it down and say, alright, enough for today.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But with people, yes… it has caused trouble.\n\nI’ve missed my walking group and the book club, and the garden’s gone all wild—my wife keeps pointing that out, the tomatoes especially, though they were never as good as my father’s used to be. I don’t mean to, but someone says one thing about a song or a photo and I’m off again.\n\nMy wife says our whole day ends up being about calming me down or steering me away from another phone call, or some trip I suddenly think I need to make, or an argument over how the display should be. I don’t like saying that, because the memorial feels important, you know, it’s for my brother, and he loved those old songs—there was one he used to hum when we were kids, though I can’t remember if it was from the radio or something my mother sang.\n\nBut yes, it’s taking a toll. I’ve missed the walking group, and book club too. The garden’s been let go, my wife keeps saying it’s getting away from us. I mean, I suppose anybody thinks about what they could’ve done differently when someone dies, don’t they? Like if the songs are wrong, or the photos are in the wrong order, then people won’t remember him the way they should. He had this laugh—my brother—and there was a picture from years ago, I think at a picnic, or maybe my cousin’s place, and I keep trying to find it.\n\nSo guilt… maybe worry, maybe pressure. It’s more that the whole thing has just grown and grown. I started with wanting a nice memorial for my brother, but now I’m spending most days chasing down the right songs, calling people, thinking about photos and displays, and then I start planning some trip to find one thing or talk to someone.\n\nAnd you know, my brother had opinions about music—he’d say the radio was all noise after a certain year, which my wife says I’ve started saying too much lately. But yes, I keep feeling like if I leave something out, it won’t be right somehow.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside—the garden, my walking group, all of it. So I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of forgetting, but I can’t seem to stop trying to get every part of it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all this for the memorial because you're afraid of forgetting your brother?\n\nPatient: Maybe… maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. What brings me in is more that the memorial has gotten bigger than I ever meant it to. I started with just wanting something nice for my brother, and then suddenly I’m spending most days chasing down songs, calling folks, planning the display, thinking I should take a trip to find this or that.\n\nAnd my wife says I’ve let everything else slide—the garden, my walking group… I used to like that walking group, there’s a fellow there who always talks about baseball, even when nobody asks him to. But I haven’t been going. It’s like everything keeps getting pulled back to the memorial.\n\nSo yes, I suppose I’m afraid of losing pieces of him, but mostly I just can’t seem to stop adding to it and working on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about the memorial even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s a good way of putting it. I’ll try to sit down and think, alright, just make a list, keep it sensible, and then my mind is off—first it’s the display case, then I’m wondering if I should drive out to find my cousin because he might have that one photo, then some line from an old ballad pops in, and I’m thinking maybe that should be read aloud.\n\nIt doesn’t stay in a straight line, if you know what I mean. My brother used to tease me about making lists and then losing the list—he had this little notebook he carried, always said I should get one. It started as something small, just a nice thing, you know, a few songs he liked—he had this old record he used to play, drove everyone nuts—but now it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to.\n\nMost days I’m calling people, tracking down music, planning displays, thinking about trips and who should be there and how it ought to look. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been going with my walking group, and… well, she says I’ve pushed just about everything else aside for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything about the memorial has to be just right?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do, or… maybe not “perfect,” but it feels like if I leave something out, I’m letting him down. So I keep chasing little pieces—songs he liked, people who should be called, how the displays ought to look, even thinking about trips for it—and then the whole day’s gone.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside. The garden’s a mess, and I haven’t been with my walking group. Funny thing, those tomatoes were actually doing pretty well this year before I got caught up in all this. But the memorial just keeps pulling me back, like there’s always one more thing I need to get right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the memorial all the time, even when you're trying to do other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s about right. I’ll be trying to do something else, and my mind just jumps—like, one minute I’m thinking about a display case, then suddenly I’m wondering if I should drive out and find this cousin nobody’s talked to in years, then I’ve got some old ballad verse stuck in my head.\n\nAnd I try to write it down so I can make sense of it, but it won’t stay in order. It’s like the thoughts are moving faster than my hand. Then I remember my brother used to hum that tune when he was fixing the radio—he was always taking things apart, drove my mother crazy—and then I’m off on that, and I’ve forgotten what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or stressed by all the work you're putting into the memorial?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed… yes, I think that’s fair. It started out as just a memorial for my brother, something simple, but now most days I’m chasing down songs, calling people, figuring out displays, even thinking about trips for it.\n\nAnd then I look up and the day’s gone. My wife says the garden’s been left, and I haven’t been seeing the walking group, which I used to like—there’s a fellow there who always tells the same joke about his knees—but anyway, yes, it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to, and it does feel like everything else has been pushed aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of the memorial yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say control every bit of it… but I do keep taking it back onto myself. Like, if someone else says they’ll call a person or pick a song, I start thinking, well, maybe they won’t remember the right version, or they won’t know what my brother would’ve liked.\n\nSo then I’m the one chasing down the songs, calling folks, planning the displays, even thinking about trips for it. And then the whole day’s gone. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been with the walking group in a while—there’s a man there who always brings peppermint candies, funny little habit—but yes, I suppose I’ve pushed most everything else aside for this.\n\nDoctor: Has your focus on the memorial caused problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Work, no—I’m not working now, so it’s not that. But with people, yes… it has caused trouble.\n\nI’ve missed my walking group and the book club, and the garden’s gone all wild—my wife keeps pointing that out, the tomatoes especially, though they were never as good as my father’s used to be. And at the senior center, I think I’ve upset a few people because I keep turning every conversation back to the memorial display. I don’t mean to, but someone says one thing about a song or a photo and I’m off again.\n\nMy wife says our whole day ends up being about calming me down or steering me away from another phone call, or some trip I suddenly think I need to make, or an argument over how the display should be. So… yes, it’s been hard on things between us and other people.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your efforts on the memorial are taking a toll on your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. I don’t like saying that, because the memorial feels important, you know, it’s for my brother, and he loved those old songs—there was one he used to hum when we were kids, though I can’t remember if it was from the radio or something my mother sang.\n\nBut yes, it’s taking a toll. I’ve missed the walking group, and book club too. The garden’s been let go, my wife keeps saying it’s getting away from us. And at the senior center, I think I’ve worn people out because everything I talk about turns into the memorial display somehow.\n\nMy wife says our days are all about settling me down or stopping me from making another call, or planning another trip, or getting into some argument about how it should look. So… yes, I suppose it’s affecting me more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about something related to your brother's death that's making you put so much into the memorial?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I suppose anybody thinks about what they could’ve done differently when someone dies, don’t they? But I don’t have some one thing, if that’s what you mean, like something I did or didn’t do.\n\nIt’s more… I keep thinking the memorial has to be right. Like if the songs are wrong, or the photos are in the wrong order, then people won’t remember him the way they should. He had this laugh—my brother—and there was a picture from years ago, I think at a picnic, or maybe my cousin’s place, and I keep trying to find it.\n\nSo guilt… maybe worry, maybe pressure. I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that if you don't include every detail in the memorial, you will forget something important about your brother?\n\nPatient: Afraid I’ll forget… yes, maybe that’s part of it. It’s more that the whole thing has just grown and grown. I started with wanting a nice memorial for my brother, but now I’m spending most days chasing down the right songs, calling people, thinking about photos and displays, and then I start planning some trip to find one thing or talk to someone.\n\nAnd you know, my brother had opinions about music—he’d say the radio was all noise after a certain year, which my wife says I’ve started saying too much lately. But yes, I keep feeling like if I leave something out, it won’t be right somehow.\n\nMy wife says I’ve pushed everything else aside—the garden, my walking group, all of it. So I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of forgetting, but I can’t seem to stop trying to get every part of it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all this for the memorial because you're afraid of forgetting your brother?\n\nPatient: Maybe… maybe that’s part of it, but I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. What brings me in is more that the memorial has gotten bigger than I ever meant it to. I started with just wanting something nice for my brother, and then suddenly I’m spending most days chasing down songs, calling folks, planning the display, thinking I should take a trip to find this or that.\n\nAnd my wife says I’ve let everything else slide—the garden, my walking group… I used to like that walking group, there’s a fellow there who always talks about baseball, even when nobody asks him to. But I haven’t been going. It’s like everything keeps getting pulled back to the memorial.\n\nSo yes, I suppose I’m afraid of losing pieces of him, but mostly I just can’t seem to stop adding to it and working on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about the memorial even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s a good way of putting it. I’ll try to sit down and think, alright, just make a list, keep it sensible, and then my mind is off—first it’s the display case, then I’m wondering if I should drive out to find my cousin because he might have that one photo, then some line from an old ballad pops in, and I’m thinking maybe that should be read aloud.\n\nIt doesn’t stay in a straight line, if you know what I mean. My brother used to tease me about making lists and then losing the list—he had this little notebook he carried, always said I should get one. But now even when I write things down, it’s like I can’t get the thoughts in order fast enough. They keep jumping ahead of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to make the memorial perfect, or is it just that you can't stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s perfect, exactly… I mean, maybe I want it right, but the bigger thing is I can’t stop the thoughts once they start.\n\nMy mind jumps all over. One minute I’m thinking about a display case, then I’m thinking I should take a road trip to find my cousin because he might know something, then some old ballad verse comes into my head and I think, no, that has to be in there too. And then I try to write it down, but by the time I’ve got the pen, I’m already on the next idea.\n\nIt’s like I can’t slow it down enough to put it in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing all this work for the memorial because you genuinely believe it needs to be this extensive, or because you just can't seem to stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t seem to stop thinking about it. I mean, I do want it to be good, of course, it’s for my brother—but it’s not like I sat down and said, “This has to be enormous.”\n\nIt’s more like my mind keeps jumping. One minute I’m thinking about a display case, then I’m thinking maybe I ought to take a road trip to find my cousin because he might have something important, and then suddenly there’s some old ballad verse in my head—my mother used to hum songs while she cooked, not always the same words, she’d mix them up—and then I think, well, maybe that belongs in the memorial too.\n\nAnd when I try to write it in order, I can’t slow it down enough. By the time I’ve written one thing, I’m already three ideas past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things for the memorial because you feel compelled to, or because you genuinely want to?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but… more like I feel pulled along by it now. I did want to do something nice for my brother, genuinely. That part’s real.\n\nBut what brings me in is that it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to. Most days I’m chasing down old songs, calling people, thinking about displays, whether I should take trips to find things… and then the day’s gone. My wife says I’ve pushed aside the garden, and my walking group, and just about everything else.\n\nI don’t know if “compelled” is the right word, but it doesn’t feel like I can just set it down and say, alright, enough for today.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop thinking about the memorial even when you try to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s exactly it. I’ll try to do something else, and then my mind just jumps. Like I’ll be thinking, maybe I should just water the tomatoes or sort the mail, and then suddenly I’m back to the display case, or whether I should drive out to find my cousin because he might know something.\n\nAnd then a bit of an old ballad comes in—my mother used to hum those, you know, while she was in the kitchen—and I think, wait, that verse matters somehow. So I go to write it down, but by the time I’ve got the paper, I’m already on to the next thing.\n\nIt’s not one thought, really. It’s like they’re all chasing each other, and I can’t slow them down enough to put them in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about the memorial that you can't get out of your head, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s it. I try to think about something else and it won’t stay there.\n\nIt’s like my mind hops tracks—first I’m picturing a display case, then I’m thinking maybe I need to drive out and find my cousin because he might remember something, and then some old ballad verse comes up. My mother used to have all these bits of songs in her head, and she’d hum them while she was cooking, and sometimes I can almost hear the tune but not quite the words…\n\nBut yes, sorry—the memorial thoughts keep coming. I can’t slow them down enough to write them out in order. By the time I put one thing on paper, I’m already off onto the next one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "role": "system",

I don’t know if I’d call them “intrusive” exactly, but I can’t ignore them, no. My mind just keeps jumping.
